Simplicial Regularizability of the Pseudo-Moment Cone and Carathéodory-Type Atomic Decomposition of Moment Matrices
Shucheng Kang, Heng Yang

Abstract
We study the facial geometry of the (homogeneous) pseudo-moment cone Σ n , 2 ​ d ∗ \mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707} and its implications for atomic decomposition of moment matrices. For fixed d ≥ 2 \mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722 , we show that if a moment matrix is formed by O ⁡ ( n d ) \mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84054785 generically chosen weighted atoms, then its minimal face in the matrix realization of the pseudo-moment cone is simplicial and generated by the planted rank-one atoms. Based on this geometric result, we develop a Carathéodory-type extreme-ray decomposition algorithm for spectrahedral cones and show that, when specialized to the pseudo-moment cone, it yields an efficient atomic decomposition method for generically generated moment matrices in the same regime. A stabilized numerical implementation demonstrates strong recovery performance, and suggests that outside the guaranteed regime the algorithm may serve as a practical sampler of high-rank extreme rays.



1
Introduction

The (homogeneous) pseudo-moment cone is a fundamental geometric object in polynomial optimization, moment theory, and convex algebraic geometry. For instance, moment relaxations of polynomial optimization problems may be formulated as linear programs over this cone
[
22
,
28
]
, while atomic decomposition of a moment matrix may be interpreted as a conic decomposition into rank-one point-evaluation rays
[
3
,
17
]
. Following
[
3
]
, we denote this cone by
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, where
\mathchar 29038
is the number of variables and
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028
is the degree.

Despite its central role, our understanding of the facial structure of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
remains limited. A major obstacle is the existence of higher-rank extreme rays—rays that do not correspond to point evaluations
[
4
]
. Existing results mainly focus on deciding the ranks of such rays and their construction
[
3
,
4
,
7
,
17
]
. In particular, there exist extreme rays of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
of rank
\mathchar 28723\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722
for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28723
and
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28723
, and of rank
\mathchar 28726
for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
and
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28724

[
4
]
. The ternary case has also been studied in greater detail
[
7
]
. While these works reveal the complexity of the global facial structure of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, it is natural to ask whether simpler local geometric structures exist that can be leveraged for algorithm design.

Contributions.

We give an affirmative answer to the above question. Our contributions are threefold.

•

Simplicial regularizability of the pseudo-moment cone.
Our main contribution is a new facial-geometric characterization of the pseudo-moment cone. For fixed
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
, we show that if a moment matrix is formed by
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84054785
generically chosen weighted atoms, then its minimal face in the matrix realization of the pseudo-moment cone is simplicial and generated by the planted rank-one atoms (
cf.
Theorem
1
). We call this property
simplicial regularizability
. Our proof is motivated by a geometric result on computing linear sections of varieties
[
20
, Theorem 15]
.

•

Carathéodory-type atomic decomposition of moment matrices.
Based on this facial-geometric result, we develop a Carathéodory-type extreme-ray decomposition procedure for spectrahedral cones; see Algorithm
1
. When specialized to
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
, the matrix realization of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, it yields an efficient atomic decomposition method for generically generated moment matrices with up to
\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84054785
atoms. This leads to an asymptotic improvement over methods based on flatness extension, whose recovery upper bound is
\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785

[
18
,
21
]
, provided that one does not solve a hierarchy of semidefinite relaxations to generate high-order moments
[
25
]
. Conceptually, our algorithm can be viewed as a practical variant of the procedure underlying the Carathéodory-number bound in
[
19
, Theorem 4]
.

•

Stabilized numerical implementation.
We develop a stabilized numerical implementation of Algorithm
1
to improve its practical robustness. Experiments demonstrate strong recovery performance, suggest that the proven recovery regime is conservative, and indicate that outside the guaranteed regime the algorithm may also serve as a practical sampler of high-rank extreme rays of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
.

Outline.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. §

reviews related work, and §

introduces the necessary notation and preliminaries. §

establishes the simplicial regularizability of the pseudo-moment cone. §

together with its stabilized implementation. Numerical experiments are given in §

, and §

2
Related Work

Facial structures of the moment cone and the pseudo-moment cone.

Prior work on pseudo-moment cones has focused mainly on extreme rays and related algebraic boundaries: the first nontrivial cases
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726\delimiter 84054785
and
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\delimiter 84054785
were characterized via Cayley–Bacharach relations
[
5
,
3
,
6
]
; positive Gorenstein ideals later provided a general framework and sharp rank thresholds for non-evaluation extreme rays
[
4
]
; and, for ternary forms, Hankel spectrahedra and their algebraic boundaries were studied systematically
[
7
]
. Exact-arithmetic constructions of pseudo-moment certificates and extreme rays were proposed more recently in
[
17
]
. On the moment-cone side, truncated moment cones were studied through atom sets, determinacy, core varieties, exposed faces, facial dimensions, and Carathéodory numbers
[
12
,
13
,
11
]
. From an optimization viewpoint,
[
27
]
exploits structured matrix representations of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, including Hankel-type structure in suitable bases, for direct SOS optimization, although not its facial geometry per se.

Atomic decomposition of representable moment matrices.

Because every representable truncated moment sequence admits a finitely atomic representing measure
[
11
]
, one is naturally led to the problem of recovering the atoms and weights from its moment matrix. This problem appears in the truncated moment problem
[
11
,
25
]
, in solution extraction for moment–SOS relaxations
[
22
,
28
,
18
]
, and in related topics such as cubature
[
14
]
and tensor decomposition
[
2
]
. A standard extraction mechanism is flat extension: once a higher-degree extension has the same rank as the original truncated moment matrix, the atoms and weights can be recovered via multiplication operators or the GNS construction
[
18
,
21
]
. This gives an a priori certificate for extraction, but with moments only up to degree
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028
, the usual flatness test limits recovery to at most
{{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\abovewithdelims( 0.0pt\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 28720\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
atoms for fixed
\mathchar 29028
. Alternatively, one may solve higher-order SDP relaxations until flatness occurs
[
25
]
; our method instead decomposes the given degree-
\mathchar 29028
moment matrix using the facial geometry of the pseudo-moment cone.

Efficient tensor decomposition algorithms.

Our facial geometry results are also related to recent progress on tensor decomposition in generic regimes. Classical linear-algebraic methods include simultaneous diagonalization and its connection to tensor decomposition
[
10
,
23
]
. Other algebraic and numerical approaches for symmetric tensors include generating-polynomial methods
[
26
]
. More recent works obtain generic recovery guarantees through planted rank-one matrices in suitable subspaces
[
20
,
9
]
, moment-matrix extension
[
31
]
, and power-iteration methods
[
34
]
. Several of these methods can also recover the atoms and weights of

\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}

in generic regimes overlapping with ours, by viewing
\mathchar 29016
as a structured symmetric tensor. Their mechanisms, however, are different: linear-section methods lift the problem to a polynomial space and use simultaneous diagonalization
[
20
]
, moment-matrix-extension methods solve for missing moments and can reduce to linear equations in favorable low-regularity regimes
[
31
]
, and power-method approaches recover components through singular-vector-type iterations
[
34
]
. By contrast, our method uses positivity and convexity to expose extreme rays of the minimal face of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
. This convex-geometric viewpoint yields a Carathéodory-type atomic decomposition and also provides a way to generate high-rank extreme rays of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, which is outside the usual target of tensor-decomposition algorithms.

Lemma 1

(Generic property under polynomial mapping)
.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
and
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
be two finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}
-vector spaces,
{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29014}
a variety, and
\mathchar 29008
a property that holds generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29016}

\mathchar 29030\mathchar 12346{\cal\mathchar 29015}\mathchar 12833{\cal\mathchar 29014}
is a polynomial mapping such that
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29015}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29016}
and
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29015}\delimiter 84054785
is Zariski dense in
{\cal\mathchar 29016}

\mathchar 29008\mathchar 8718\mathchar 29030
holds generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
.

Proof.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29016}
be a Zariski-open dense subset on which
\mathchar 29008
holds. Since
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29015}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29016}

\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 84054785
is well-defined. Moreover, because
\mathchar 29030
is a polynomial mapping, it is continuous in the Zariski topology, so
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 84054785
is Zariski open in
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
. Since
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29015}\delimiter 84054785
is Zariski dense in
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
and
{\cal\mathchar 28993}
is Zariski open dense in
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
, we have
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29015}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 571
, and hence
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\mathchar 571
. Finally, because
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
is irreducible in the Zariski topology, every nonempty Zariski-open subset of
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
is dense. Therefore,
\mathchar 29030^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 84054785
is Zariski open dense in
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
, which shows that
\mathchar 29008\mathchar 8718\mathchar 29030
holds generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29015}
.
∎

Lemma 2

(Generic property under finite conjunction)
.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
be a finite-dimensional
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}
-vector space and
{\cal\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29014}
an irreducible variety. If properties
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28721}
and
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28722}
both hold generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
, then
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8798\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28722}
also holds generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
.

Proof.

By definition, there exist Zariski-open dense subsets
{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12818{\cal\mathchar 29016}
such that
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28721}
holds on
{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28721}
and
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28722}
holds on
{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28722}
. Hence,
{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28722}
is Zariski open in
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
. Since
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
is irreducible, every nonempty Zariski-open subset of
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
is dense, so
{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 28722}
is Zariski open dense in
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
. On this set, both
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28721}
and
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28722}
hold, and therefore
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8798\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 28722}
holds generically on
{\cal\mathchar 29016}
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
1
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We prove the theorem in five steps. First, we apply Theorem
2
with
{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 12349\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}}\delimiter 84054785
and
\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
, obtaining a generic avoidance statement for the mixed tensors
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8799\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29034}
,
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29034
. Second, we pull this statement back to the affine parameters
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
via the polynomial map
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathrel{\mathchar 567\mathchar 545}\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\tilde{\mathchar 29050}_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 8714\mathchar 29028}
, where
\tilde{\mathchar 29050}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24635\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84267779
. Third, we define

Theorem
1
should be viewed as a local facial statement rather than a global description of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
. For fixed
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
and
\mathchar 29043
in the regime (
7
), it shows that a moment matrix formed by
\mathchar 29043
generically chosen weighted atoms lies on a simplicial minimal face generated by the planted rank-one atomic rays, so that the cone locally behaves like
\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29043}
. In this sense, although the pseudo-moment cone may be globally complicated, it
regularizes
around such matrices to a cone with transparent facial structure. This does not contradict the known existence of higher-rank extreme rays in
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
, since those results concern the ambient cone globally, whereas Theorem
1
concerns only the minimal face containing moment matrices of this particular form. This local simplicial structure is exactly the geometric mechanism that later enables the extreme-ray decomposition procedure to recover the planted atoms and weights uniquely up to reordering, as we will discuss in §
5.2
.

5
Carathéodory-Type Atomic Decomposition of Moment Matrices

In this section, we study the properties of Algorithm
1
in detail. In §
5.1
, we prove that, under exact arithmetic, Algorithm
1
almost surely produces an extreme-ray decomposition for any given pair
\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785
. In §
5.2
, we further show that, when
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
and
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, Algorithm
1
can almost surely recover, up to a proper reordering, the atoms
\{\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29043}
and weights
\{\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29043}
, provided that the conditions of Theorem
1
hold.
In §
5.3
, we introduce several techniques to stabilize Algorithm
1
under finite-precision arithmetic, leading to the practical Algorithm
2
. Finally, in §
5.4
, we estimate the time and memory complexity of Algorithm
2
for the case
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
.

5.1
Correctness of Algorithm
1
under Exact Arithmetic

We first provide some elementary lemmas on convex geometry.

Lemma 9

(Faces of a spectrahedral cone)
.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29004}\mathchar 8796\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}

{\cal\mathchar 29004}\mathchar 12818\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}

{\cal\mathchar 28998}

by
[

, Theorem 3.3.1(2)(b)]
. Moreover,

\mathchar 29042

Lemma 10

(Extreme points and extreme rays)
.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12818\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}
be a nonempty spectrahedral cone, and define
{\cal\mathchar 28994}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 8796\{\mathchar 29017\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12906\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\}
. Then
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}
is an extreme point of
{\cal\mathchar 28994}
iff
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
is an extreme ray of
{\cal\mathchar 28995}

Proof.

Since every nonzero element of
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
is PSD, it has positive trace. Thus every nonzero ray of
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
intersects
{\cal\mathchar 28994}
in exactly one point. Suppose first that
\mathchar 29016
is an extreme point of
{\cal\mathchar 28994}
, and let
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29017\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018
with
\mathchar 29017\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28995}
. If both
\mathchar 29017
and
\mathchar 29018
are nonzero, then
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785
, and so
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785{{\mathchar 29017\over\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785}}\mathchar 8235\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785{{\mathchar 29018\over\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785}}
, where
{{\mathchar 29017\over\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785}}\mathchar 24891{{\mathchar 29018\over\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785}}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}
. Since
\mathchar 29016
is an extreme point of
{\cal\mathchar 28994}
, both normalized matrices must equal
\mathchar 29016
. Hence
\mathchar 29017\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
, and therefore
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
is an extreme ray of
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
. Conversely, suppose that
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
is an extreme ray of
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
, and let
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28949\mathchar 29017\mathchar 8235\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29018
with
\mathchar 29017\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28994}
and
\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28949\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28721
. Then
\mathchar 28949\mathchar 29017\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28995}
. By extremality of
{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
, we have
\mathchar 29017\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29016
. Since
\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29017\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
, it follows that
\mathchar 29017\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29016
. Thus
\mathchar 29016
is an extreme point of
{\cal\mathchar 28994}
.
∎

Now we are ready to show that, for a general spectrahedral cone
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
, Algorithm
1
produces, with probability
\mathchar 28721
, an extreme-ray decomposition of any nonzero
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28995}
.

Theorem 3

(Algorithm
1
generates extreme rays)
.

Let
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12818\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}

5.2
Simplicial Regularizability Enforces Uniqueness of Decomposition

As one may have already noticed, in (
1
), different choices of the linear functional
\mathchar 28994_{\mathchar 29035}
may produce different extreme rays
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}
even when
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathchar 29035}
is fixed. In other words, although Algorithm
1
almost surely returns an extreme ray decomposition of
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28995}
, the decomposition need not be unique. For the same input
\mathchar 29016
, even the number of iterations
\mathchar 29042
may vary, let alone the collection
\{\mathchar 29044_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29042}
. For example, if
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}
and
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 8235}
, then
\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29030\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29029}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}
, and minimizing a random linear functional may attain any rank-
\mathchar 28721
extreme ray of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}

i.e.,

{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}_{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 29048^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 84054785
for arbitrary nonzero
\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}^{\mathchar 29038}
.

In the special case where
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29037_{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
and
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
, however, the situation is markedly different. Indeed, as long as
\mathchar 29043
satisfies (
7
) and
\{\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29050_{\mathchar 29033}\}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29043}
is chosen generically from

\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29030\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29029}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 84054785
becomes a simplicial cone from Theorem

. The following lemma shows that, with probability
\mathchar 28721

Lemma 11

(Random linear functional selects a generator of a simplicial cone)
.

Let

Now we combine Theorem

Theorem 4

We note that Algorithm
1
and Corollary
1
can recover asymptotically more atoms than direct flatness-based extraction from the given moment matrix
[
18
,
21
]
. For fixed
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28722
, the usual flatness test at degree
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028
can certify and extract at most
\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
atoms, while providing an
a priori
sufficient rank condition for existence and recovery. This limitation can be bypassed by hierarchy-based approaches that complete higher-order moments until a flat extension is found
[
25
]
. In contrast, our method decomposes the given degree-
\mathchar 29028
moment matrix directly, using the simplicial regularizability of the pseudo-moment cone, and allows recovery of up to
\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84054785
atoms. The tradeoff is that our method does not currently come with an analogous
a priori
sufficient certificate.

5.3
Stabilizing Algorithm
1
under Finite-Precision Arithmetic

Theorem
3
, Theorem
4
, and Corollary
1
are all established under the assumption that (
1
) and (
2
) can be solved in exact arithmetic. Unfortunately, this assumption is unrealistic in practice. In fact, a direct implementation of Algorithm
1
typically becomes numerically unstable after only
\mathchar 28722
or
\mathchar 28723
iterations due to the accumulation of various numerical errors.

To address this issue, we develop several techniques to stabilize Algorithm
1
, including (i) facial reduction to improve the stability of the SDP solved in (
1
); (ii) a closed-form expression for
\mathchar 29044_{\mathchar 29035}
in (
2
); (iii) an alternating projection subroutine to improve feasibility; and (iv) a restarting mechanism to automatically handle SDP failures and accumulated numerical error. Throughout this section, we represent the spectrahedral cone
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12818\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}
as
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 8796\{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\}
, where the linear map
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12833{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
is defined by
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 0\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 574}
with
\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}
for each
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779
. We denote by
\mathrm{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
the vectorization of
\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29033}
, and by

(i) Facial reduction.

A major source of instability in Algorithm
1
is that, when
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29006
, solving (
1
) directly in the ambient space
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}
ignores that the feasible set is confined to a proper face of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}_{\mathchar 8235}
. In this case, strict feasibility in the ambient cone may fail, along with the regularity properties typically exploited by SDP solvers, including strong duality
[
29
]
. The effect can be particularly severe when
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12828\mathchar 29006
. To address this issue, we apply facial reduction to restore strict feasibility on the relevant face
[
8
]
; see Lines
26
–
31
of Algorithm
2
. Specifically, we compute the numerical rank
\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}
of
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}
and an orthonormal basis
\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}
for its column space, both with tolerance
\mathchar 28962_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}}
, and restrict the subproblem to the face
\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
. We then remove numerically redundant constraints, up to tolerance
\mathchar 28962_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036}}
, using a pivoted QR factorization (
cf.
Algorithm
3
). The resulting regularized linear SDP has size
\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}
and only
\left\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 69640972
independent constraints. Since its feasible set satisfies Slater’s condition and is compact, the primal and dual optimal values coincide, and both optima are attained.

(ii) Closed-form expression for
\mathchar 29044_{\mathchar 29035}
.

The scalar
\mathchar 29044_{\mathchar 29035}
in (
2
) admits a closed-form expression. Let

\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}

be the eigenvalue decomposition of
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}
at the
\mathchar 29035
-th iteration of Algorithm
1
, where
\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29006\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}}
has orthonormal columns and
\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 8235}
is diagonal. Since
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29030\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29029}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 84054785
, we must also have

\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}

for some
\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathchar 29044_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349

\displaystyle\sup\{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathchar 29035}\}\mathchar 12349\sup\{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12906\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29044\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235}\}

\displaystyle\sup\{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29001_{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29044\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235}\}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28949_{\max}^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\bigl\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}\bigr\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(28)

The second equality holds because both
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}
and
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}
satisfy the linear constraints defining
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
, so
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\}
for every
\mathchar 29044
; hence only the PSD constraint remains. The final equality is well defined because
\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\delimiter 67273472\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
and
\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}}_{\mathchar 8235}
, which implies
\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}\widetilde{\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29035}\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}}
is nonzero and positive semidefinite. We have therefore replaced (
2
) by (
5.3
) in Algorithm
2
, Line
36
.

(iii) Alternating projection.

In exact arithmetic,
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 28995}
at every iteration of Algorithm
1
. In practice, however, the SDP subproblems are not solved to machine precision, so the iterates gradually drift away from
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
, which can destabilize subsequent subproblems. To mitigate this effect, we apply a cheap alternating projection step (
cf.
Algorithm
4
) after each iteration to improve the feasibility of
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}
with respect to
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
.
The alternating projection routine is terminated after at most
\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29044}}
steps, or earlier if the stopping criterion with tolerance
\mathchar 28962_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29044}}
is satisfied.
Since
{\cal\mathchar 28993}
is typically sparse, the projection onto
\{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\}
in Line
5
can be implemented efficiently in two stages: we first precompute a sparse Cholesky factorization of
{\cal\mathchar 28993}{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
at the beginning of Algorithm
2
,
and then, at each projection step, solve two sparse triangular systems. In practice, the cost of alternating projection is usually negligible compared with the SDP solve. A more detailed complexity discussion is deferred to §
5.4
.

(iv) A restarting mechanism.

To further improve robustness, we restart the extreme-ray decomposition whenever an SDP subproblem cannot be solved reliably. In practice, such failures are often caused by overly aggressive eigenvalue truncation in Line
26
of Algorithm
2
, which may render the reduced SDP in Line
31
infeasible. Accordingly, if the inner routine
RayDecompFR
(
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
19
) returns no extreme rays,
i.e.,
when the SDP solver fails at the first iteration, we reduce
\mathchar 28962_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}}
by a factor of
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
(
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
12
). More generally, whenever an SDP failure is detected, we first project the current iterate back onto
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
(
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
13
) using alternating projection (
cf.
Algorithm
4
), and then restart the procedure.

5.4
Estimation of Complexity

Now we estimate the time and memory complexity of Algorithm
2
. We begin with a general spectrahedral cone
{\cal\mathchar 28995}
represented by
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12833{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
, and then specialize to
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
.

Since each generated
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29035}
decreases the iterate’s rank by at least
\mathchar 28721
, it is reasonable, by amortized analysis, to regard the total number of inner-loop iterations in
RayDecompFR
(
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
23
to
37
) as
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042_{\max}\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29042_{\max}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29006
is the numerical rank of the input matrix
\mathchar 29016
. Within each inner-loop iteration, the four main sources of complexity are: (i) eigenvalue decomposition to compute numerical rank (
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
23
); (ii) SDP solve to generate an extreme ray (
cf.
Algorithm
2
, Line
31
); (iii) pivoted QR decomposition to remove redundant constraints in facial reduction (
cf.
Algorithm
3
); and (iv) alternating projection to improve feasibility (
cf.
Algorithm
4
).

At iteration
\mathchar 29035
, the iterate
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29035}
has numerical rank
\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29042_{\max}
. Computing its eigenvalue decomposition costs
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
time and
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
memory. Solving the reduced SDP by an interior-point method costs
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28726}\delimiter 84054785
time and
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28724}\delimiter 84054785
memory, where
\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29037}}
denotes the number of IPM iterations and is typically moderate
[
32
]
. The pivoted QR step takes as input a matrix of size
\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8706\left\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 69640972
, and therefore costs
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\min\{\mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28724}\left\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29042_{\mathchar 29035}^{\mathchar 28722}\left\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 69640972^{\mathchar 28722}\}\delimiter 84054785

memory. By definition, we can upper bound
\left\delimiter 69640972{\cal\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 69640972
by
\mathchar 29037
. The complexity of alternating projection depends on the sparsity pattern of
{\cal\mathchar 28993}
, so we now specialize to the pseudo-moment cone

In this case,

In summary, for
{\cal\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}

is bounded by

\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 29042_{\max}^{\mathchar 28726}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29042_{\max}^{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

and the corresponding memory complexity is bounded by

Since the total number of inner-loop iterations is
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042_{\max}\delimiter 84054785
, the overall time complexity of Algorithm

\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042_{\max}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 29042_{\max}^{\mathchar 28726}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29042_{\max}^{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29012_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 29006^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

In the worst case

6
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we evaluate the practical efficiency and numerical robustness of Algorithm
2
under finite-precision arithmetic. In §
6.1
, we assess its performance as a numerical atomic decomposition method for moment matrices. In §
6.2
, we examine regimes in which the algorithm no longer recovers the original weighted atoms, yet still produces a collection of generally non-unique extreme rays of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
. In particular, the experiments reveal the emergence of high-rank extreme rays, suggesting that Algorithm
2
may also serve as a practical sampler for at least a subset of the high-rank extreme rays of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
. All experiments were carried out on a high-performance workstation equipped with a 2.7 GHz AMD 64-Core sWRX8 processor and 1 TB of RAM. The implementation and experimental results are available at
https://github.com/ComputationalRobotics/momentcone-simregularity
.

6.1
Algorithm
2
as Atomic Decomposition of Moment Matrix

The hyper-parameters in Algorithm
2
are fixed throughout the experiments as
\mathchar 28962_{\mathsf{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28727}

Results.

We consider two choices of the relaxation order
\mathchar 29028
: (i) for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
, we sweep
\mathchar 29038
from
\mathchar 28723
to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
; and (ii) for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723
, we sweep
\mathchar 29038
from
\mathchar 28722
to
\mathchar 28725
. The cases
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
and
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785
correspond to
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724}
and
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 28723\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726}
, respectively, the two smallest cases in which the SOS cone differs from the cone of nonnegative forms
[
6
]
. Figure
1
reports
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29047}
and
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29050}
as functions of
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006\delimiter 84267779
. For each
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
, we observe a clear phase transition: below an empirical threshold, both recovery errors remain essentially zero, whereas above this threshold they rapidly increase to
\mathchar 28721
. Table
1
summarizes these empirical phase-transition thresholds and compares them with the theoretical upper bound on
\mathchar 29043
from (
7
). The empirical thresholds are consistently much larger than the theoretical ones, suggesting that the bound in (
7
) is conservative in practice. Finally, Table
2
reports the running time for a single sample
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
with
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29028\abovewithdelims( 0.0pt\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 28720\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29028}}
, illustrating the efficiency of the implementation.

6.2
Algorithm
2
as Sampler of High-Rank Extreme Rays of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}

We now report an intriguing empirical phenomenon: once
\mathchar 29043
crosses the phase-transition threshold,
i.e.,
once it enters the regime in which both
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29047}
and
\mathchar 29029_{\mathchar 29050}
are nearly
\mathchar 28721
in Figure
1
, high-rank extreme rays of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
begin to appear. For example, when
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
, Figure
2
shows snapshots of the numerical ranks of the extreme rays returned from
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
random initial matrices
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 29033}
for different values of
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28723\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006\delimiter 84267779
. When
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28726
, all returned extreme rays have rank
\mathchar 28721
. Once
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28727
, however, rank-
\mathchar 28726
extreme rays appear for almost all random seeds. This observation is consistent with the theoretical results in
[
3
,
6
]
, which show that the extreme rays of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724}^{\mathchar 8707}
have rank either
\mathchar 28721
or
\mathchar 28726
. The numerical ranks
\mathchar 28723
and
\mathchar 28725
observed when
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28728
are artifacts of finite-precision rank truncation; they should be interpreted as rank-
\mathchar 28721
and rank-
\mathchar 28726
extreme rays, respectively.

A similar pattern is observed for other
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
pairs. We refer the reader to our
codebase
for animations of how the extreme-ray ranks evolve as
\mathchar 29043
increases. Figure
3
presents snapshots of the numerical ranks of the returned extreme rays when
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29006
. From these plots, we read off the following rank ranges: (a)

\mathchar 28728\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28729

\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722
–
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28724

(with
\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28725
and
\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28726
absent). At the same time, our current algorithm does not appear to sample the full spectrum of extreme rays of
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29043}}
. Indeed,
[
4
]
shows that rank-
\mathchar 28726
extreme rays exist for all
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
and
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28724
, yet no such rays appear in Figure
3
.
Another interesting empirical observation is that, within a single decomposition trajectory, higher-rank extreme rays
always
appear only after lower-rank ones. For example, when
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\delimiter 84054785
, Algorithm
2
typically encounters a sequence of rank-
\mathchar 28721
extreme rays first, followed by rank-
\mathchar 28728
extreme rays, and finally rank-
\mathchar 28729
extreme rays. This consistent ordering suggests that the algorithm may traverse extreme rays in a structured manner, with lower-rank rays appearing earlier in the decomposition and higher-rank rays emerging only at later stages.
Explaining these empirical phenomena would be an interesting direction for future work.

7
Conclusion

We established a new facial-geometric description of the pseudo-moment cone via simplicial regularizability around generically generated moment matrices. Based on this structure, we developed a Carathéodory-type extreme-ray decomposition method for spectrahedral cones and showed that, for pseudo-moment cones, it gives an efficient atomic decomposition algorithm recovering generically generated moment matrices with up to
\mathchar 28674\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 84054785
atoms. We further introduced a stabilized implementation and demonstrated strong practical performance in numerical experiments, including the appearance of high-rank extreme rays beyond the proven regime. It would be interesting to extend this framework to broader term-sparsity settings
[
33
]
and non-commutative settings
[
21
]
, and to further improve the efficiency of Algorithm
2
while clarifying its role as a sampler of high-rank extreme rays of
\mathchar 28678_{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29028}^{\mathchar 8707}
.

Acknowledgments

We are grateful to Grigoriy Blekherman and Pablo A. Parrilo for valuable discussions on high-rank extreme rays of the pseudo-moment cone. We also thank Joseph Kileel and Bobby Shi for valuable discussions on tensor decomposition. Part of this work was conducted during visits by SK and HY to the Polynomial Optimization team at LAAS-CNRS, where Jean-Bernard Lasserre, Didier Henrion, and Victor Magron shared helpful insights.
